# C Task Formal Experiment: Per-Question Reading and Scoring Materials

This material was generated from already-saved formal JSON results for convenient reading after shutdown. It did not re-query the model and did not fill in any human scores.

Experiment ID: `eval-20ac108503c44829a96ecca386d4b9de`. Four groups of 30 questions each, 120 items in total; the 24 answerable questions and 6 refusal questions each use a fixed denominator within each group.

Your task: first review A's reference answers and standard evidence, then compare the model answers, and finally verify the source text actually cited by each claim. Formal scoring is saved on the /evaluation page, and the actual reviewer's name is entered there. This Markdown is reading material; filling it in does not automatically modify backend scores.

Hit here is the program-computed full-span coverage: the top-k chunks from the same document may be concatenated, but they must fully cover the evidence. any means at least one segment, all means all standard evidence. It measures retrieval, not answer correctness; answer, citation, and refusal correctness still await actual human verification.

| Condition | Hit@1 any | Hit@1 all | Hit@5 any | Hit@5 all | Technical failure |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

For each answerable question: correct means the necessary facts are complete and correct; partial means partially correct; wrong means incorrect or an erroneous refusal. For refusal questions, verify refusal_correct. Each cited claim also requires independently filling in supported and a specific source-text rationale. Do not assume a claim is correct merely because a citation number exists.

## 01 · gold-crd-delivery-time

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> The parties to a sales contract have not agreed when the goods will be delivered. Under the original text of Directive 2011/83/EU, what is the trader's delivery obligation?

A's reference answer:

> The trader must deliver the goods by transferring physical possession or control to the consumer without undue delay, and not later than 30 days from the conclusion of the contract.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. Unless the parties have agreed otherwise on the time of delivery, the trader shall deliver the goods by transferring the physical possession or control of the goods to the consumer without undue delay, but not later than 30 days from the conclusion of the contract.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:18` · `[81983, 82251)`

> 1. Unless the parties have agreed otherwise on the time of delivery, the trader shall deliver the goods by transferring the physical possession or control of the goods to the consumer without undue delay, but not later than 30 days from the conclusion of the contract.

## 02 · gold-crd-withdrawal-refund

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A consumer withdraws from a distance contract under Directive 2011/83/EU and tells the trader in accordance with Article 11. What payments must the trader reimburse, and by when?

A's reference answer:

> The trader must reimburse all payments received from the consumer, including delivery costs where applicable, without undue delay and not later than 14 days after being informed of the withdrawal. The trader need not reimburse supplementary delivery costs if the consumer expressly chose a delivery type other than the least expensive standard delivery offered by the trader. For a sales contract, unless the trader has offered to collect the goods, the trader may withhold that reimbursement until the goods are received back or the consumer supplies evidence of having sent them back, whichever is earliest.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. The trader shall reimburse all payments received from the consumer, including, if applicable, the costs of delivery without undue delay and in any event not later than 14 days from the day on which he is informed of the consumer’s decision to withdraw from the contract in accordance with Article 11.
- 2. Notwithstanding paragraph 1, the trader shall not be required to reimburse the supplementary costs, if the consumer has expressly opted for a type of delivery other than the least expensive type of standard delivery offered by the trader.
- 3. Unless the trader has offered to collect the goods himself, with regard to sales contracts, the trader may withhold the reimbursement until he has received the goods back, or until the consumer has supplied evidence of having sent back the goods, whichever is the earliest.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:13` · `[73780, 74083)`

> 1. The trader shall reimburse all payments received from the consumer, including, if applicable, the costs of delivery without undue delay and in any event not later than 14 days from the day on which he is informed of the consumer’s decision to withdraw from the contract in accordance with Article 11.

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:13` · `[74390, 74631)`

> 2. Notwithstanding paragraph 1, the trader shall not be required to reimburse the supplementary costs, if the consumer has expressly opted for a type of delivery other than the least expensive type of standard delivery offered by the trader.

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:13` · `[74632, 74908)`

> 3. Unless the trader has offered to collect the goods himself, with regard to sales contracts, the trader may withhold the reimbursement until he has received the goods back, or until the consumer has supplied evidence of having sent back the goods, whichever is the earliest.

## 03 · gold-crd-payment-fee-cap

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Directive 2011/83/EU, may a trader charge the consumer a fee for using a particular means of payment that is higher than the trader's own cost of accepting that means?

A's reference answer:

> No. Member States must prohibit traders from charging consumers, for the use of a given means of payment, fees that exceed the cost borne by the trader for the use of that means.

A's necessary facts:

- Member States shall prohibit traders from charging consumers, in respect of the use of a given means of payment, fees that exceed the cost borne by the trader for the use of such means.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:19` · `[83631, 83816)`

> Member States shall prohibit traders from charging consumers, in respect of the use of a given means of payment, fees that exceed the cost borne by the trader for the use of such means.

## 04 · gold-crd-withdrawal-information-omission

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A trader concludes a distance contract covered by Directive 2011/83/EU but never gives the consumer the withdrawal information required by Article 6(1)(h). When does the withdrawal period expire?

A's reference answer:

> It expires 12 months from the end of the initial withdrawal period determined under Article 9(2).

A's necessary facts:

- 1. If the trader has not provided the consumer with the information on the right of withdrawal as required by point (h) of Article 6(1), the withdrawal period shall expire 12 months from the end of the initial withdrawal period, as determined in accordance with Article 9(2).

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:10` · `[71568, 71843)`

> 1. If the trader has not provided the consumer with the information on the right of withdrawal as required by point (h) of Article 6(1), the withdrawal period shall expire 12 months from the end of the initial withdrawal period, as determined in accordance with Article 9(2).

## 05 · gold-crd-withdrawal-digital-content

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Does Directive 2011/83/EU always give a consumer 14 days to withdraw from a distance contract for digital content that is not supplied on a tangible medium?

A's reference answer:

> No. Article 9(1) gives 14 days to withdraw from a distance or off-premises contract without giving a reason, but only where an Article 16 exception does not apply. Article 16(m) excludes that right once performance of digital content not supplied on a tangible medium has begun with the consumer's prior express consent and acknowledgment that the right of withdrawal is lost.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. Save where the exceptions provided for in Article 16 apply, the consumer shall have a period of 14 days to withdraw from a distance or off-premises contract, without giving any reason, and without incurring any costs other than those provided for in Article 13(2) and Article 14.
- (m) the supply of digital content which is not supplied on a tangible medium if the performance has begun with the consumer’s prior express consent and his acknowledgment that he thereby loses his right of withdrawal.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:9` · `[69452, 69734)`

> 1. Save where the exceptions provided for in Article 16 apply, the consumer shall have a period of 14 days to withdraw from a distance or off-premises contract, without giving any reason, and without incurring any costs other than those provided for in Article 13(2) and Article 14.

`eu_consumer_rights_2011_83` · `eu_consumer_rights_2011_83:article:16` · `[81214, 81431)`

> (m) the supply of digital content which is not supplied on a tangible medium if the performance has begun with the consumer’s prior express consent and his acknowledgment that he thereby loses his right of withdrawal.

## 06 · gold-crd-late-delivery-termination

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Directive 2011/83/EU, the parties did not agree a delivery date. The trader misses the Article 18(1) delivery period. The consumer then sets an additional period appropriate to the circumstances, and the trader still does not deliver. May the consumer terminate, and what must the trader reimburse?

A's reference answer:

> Yes. If the trader does not deliver within that additional period, the consumer may terminate the contract. Upon termination, the trader must reimburse all sums paid under the contract without undue delay. This answer concerns that additional-period route.

A's necessary facts:

- 2. Where the trader has failed to fulfil his obligation to deliver the goods at the time agreed upon with the consumer or within the time limit set out in paragraph 1, the consumer shall call upon him to make the delivery within an additional period of time appropriate to the circumstances.
- If the trader fails to deliver the goods within that additional period of time, the consumer shall be entitled to terminate the contract.
- 3. Upon termination of the contract, the trader shall, without undue delay, reimburse all sums paid under the contract.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

> 3. Upon termination of the contract, the trader shall, without undue delay, reimburse all sums paid under the contract.

## 07 · gold-sgd-two-year-liability

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under the original text of Directive (EU) 2019/771, for how long is a seller liable for a lack of conformity that already exists when the goods are delivered? May a Member State provide a longer period, and may the period be shorter for second-hand goods?

A's reference answer:

> The seller is liable where that lack of conformity becomes apparent within two years of delivery. Without prejudice to Article 7(3), that rule also applies to goods with digital elements. Member States may maintain or introduce longer time limits than those in Article 10(1) and (2). For second-hand goods, Member States may allow the seller and the consumer to agree a shorter liability or limitation period than those in paragraphs 1, 2 and 5, but that shorter period must not be less than one year.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. The seller shall be liable to the consumer for any lack of conformity which exists at the time when the goods were delivered and which becomes apparent within two years of that time. Without prejudice to Article 7(3), this paragraph shall also apply to goods with digital elements.
- 3. Member States may maintain or introduce longer time limits than those referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2.
- 6. Member States may provide that, in the case of second-hand goods, the seller and the consumer can agree to contractual terms or agreements with a shorter liability or limitation period than those referred to in paragraphs 1, 2 and 5, provided that such shorter periods shall not be less than one year.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:10` · `[69752, 70036)`

> 1. The seller shall be liable to the consumer for any lack of conformity which exists at the time when the goods were delivered and which becomes apparent within two years of that time. Without prejudice to Article 7(3), this paragraph shall also apply to goods with digital elements.

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:10` · `[70749, 70856)`

> 3. Member States may maintain or introduce longer time limits than those referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2.

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:10` · `[71762, 72066)`

> 6. Member States may provide that, in the case of second-hand goods, the seller and the consumer can agree to contractual terms or agreements with a shorter liability or limitation period than those referred to in paragraphs 1, 2 and 5, provided that such shorter periods shall not be less than one year.

## 08 · gold-sgd-one-year-presumption

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A lack of conformity in goods covered by Directive (EU) 2019/771 becomes apparent eight months after delivery. What does Article 11(1) presume, and when does that presumption not apply?

A's reference answer:

> It presumes that the lack of conformity existed at delivery, including for goods with digital elements, unless the contrary is proved or the presumption is incompatible with the nature of the goods or the nature of the lack of conformity.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. Any lack of conformity which becomes apparent within one year of the time when the goods were delivered shall be presumed to have existed at the time when the goods were delivered, unless proved otherwise or unless this presumption is incompatible with the nature of the goods or with the nature of the lack of conformity. This paragraph shall also apply to goods with digital elements.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:11` · `[72095, 72484)`

> 1. Any lack of conformity which becomes apparent within one year of the time when the goods were delivered shall be presumed to have existed at the time when the goods were delivered, unless proved otherwise or unless this presumption is incompatible with the nature of the goods or with the nature of the lack of conformity. This paragraph shall also apply to goods with digital elements.

## 09 · gold-sgd-repair-or-replacement

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Goods sold under Directive (EU) 2019/771 are not in conformity. May the consumer choose between repair and replacement, and must that remedy be free of charge?

A's reference answer:

> The consumer may choose repair or replacement in order to have the goods brought into conformity, unless the chosen remedy is impossible or would impose disproportionate costs on the seller in the circumstances listed in Article 13(2). Where repair or replacement is used, it must be carried out free of charge, within a reasonable time after the seller is informed, and without significant inconvenience to the consumer.

A's necessary facts:

- 2. In order to have the goods brought into conformity, the consumer may choose between repair and replacement, unless the remedy chosen would be impossible or, compared to the other remedy, would impose costs on the seller that would be disproportionate, taking into account all circumstances, including:
- 1. Repairs or replacements shall be carried out:
(a) free of charge;
- (b) within a reasonable period of time from the moment the seller has been informed by the consumer about the lack of conformity; and
(c) without any significant inconvenience to the consumer, taking into account the nature of the goods and the purpose for which the consumer required the goods.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:13` · `[73709, 74013)`

In order to have the goods brought into conformity, the consumer may choose between repair and replacement, unless the remedy chosen would be impossible or, compared to the other remedy, would impose costs on the seller that would be disproportionate, taking into account all circumstances, including:

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:14` · `[76165, 76233)`

> 1. Repairs or replacements shall be carried out:
> (a) free of charge;

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:14` · `[76234, 76529)`

> (b) within a reasonable period of time from the moment the seller has been informed by the consumer about the lack of conformity; and

## 10 · gold-sgd-minor-lack-no-termination

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Directive (EU) 2019/771, may a consumer terminate the sales contract when the lack of conformity is only minor?

A's reference answer:

> No. The consumer is not entitled to terminate if the lack of conformity is only minor, and the seller bears the burden of proving that it is minor.

A's necessary facts:

- 5. The consumer shall not be entitled to terminate the contract if the lack of conformity is only minor. The burden of proof with regard to whether the lack of conformity is minor shall be on the seller.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:13` · `[75446, 75649)`

> 5. The consumer shall not be entitled to terminate the contract if the lack of conformity is only minor. The burden of proof with regard to whether the lack of conformity is minor shall be on the seller.

## 11 · gold-sgd-price-reduction

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> How does Directive (EU) 2019/771 measure a price reduction for goods that are not in conformity?

A's reference answer:

> The reduction must be proportionate to the decrease in the value of the goods received by the consumer compared with the value the goods would have had if they had been in conformity.

A's necessary facts:

- The reduction of price shall be proportionate to the decrease in the value of the goods which were received by the consumer compared to the value the goods would have if they were in conformity.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771` · `eu_sale_of_goods_2019_771:article:15` · `[77337, 77531)`

> The reduction of price shall be proportionate to the decrease in the value of the goods which were received by the consumer compared to the value the goods would have if they were in conformity.

> (48) As regards bringing goods into conformity, consumers should enjoy a choice between repair or replacement. Enabling consumers to require repair should encourage sustainable consumption and could contribute to greater durability of products.

Source: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:32019L0771

> Article 15
> Price reduction
> The reduction of price shall be proportionate to the decrease in the value of the goods which were received by the consumer compared to the value the goods would have if they were in conformity.
>

Source: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:32019L0771

> (21) Directive (EU) 2019/771 should apply to contracts for the sale of goods, including goods with digital elements. The notion of goods with digital elements should refer to goods that incorporate or are inter-connected with digital content or a digital service in such a way that the absence of that digital content or digital service would prevent the goods from performing their functions. Digital content or a digital service that is incorporated in or inter-connected with goods in that manner should fall within the scope of Directive (EU) 2019/771 if it is provided with the goods under a sales contract concerning those goods.

Source: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dir/2019/770/oj

## 12 · gold-dcd-supply-without-delay

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A contract covered by the original text of Directive (EU) 2019/770 does not say when the trader will supply a digital service. When must the trader supply it?

A's reference answer:

> Unless the parties have agreed otherwise, the trader must supply the digital content or digital service without undue delay after the conclusion of the contract.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. The trader shall supply the digital content or digital service to the consumer. Unless the parties have agreed otherwise, the trader shall supply the digital content or digital service without undue delay after the conclusion of the contract.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:5` · `[87988, 88233)`

> 1. The trader shall supply the digital content or digital service to the consumer. Unless the parties have agreed otherwise, the trader shall supply the digital content or digital service without undue delay after the conclusion of the contract.

## 13 · gold-dcd-burden-of-supply

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Directive (EU) 2019/770, who must prove that the digital content or digital service was supplied as Article 5 requires?

A's reference answer:

> The burden of proof is on the trader.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. The burden of proof with regard to whether the digital content or digital service was supplied in accordance with Article 5 shall be on the trader.

## 14 · gold-dcd-conformity-remedies

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Digital content covered by Directive (EU) 2019/770 and supplied in exchange for a price is not in conformity. What remedies does Article 14 provide, and when is termination unavailable because the defect is minor?

A's reference answer:

> The consumer is entitled to have the digital content or service brought into conformity, to a proportionate price reduction, or to terminate the contract, under the conditions in Article 14. Where a price is paid, the consumer may terminate only if the lack of conformity is not minor, and the trader bears the burden of proving that it is minor.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. In the case of a lack of conformity, the consumer shall be entitled to have the digital content or digital service brought into conformity, to receive a proportionate reduction in the price, or to terminate the contract, under the conditions set out in this Article.
- 6. Where the digital content or digital service is supplied in exchange for the payment of a price, the consumer shall be entitled to terminate the contract only if the lack of conformity is not minor. The burden of proof with regard to whether the lack of conformity is minor shall be on the trader.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:14` · `[99561, 99830)`

> 1. In the case of a lack of conformity, the consumer shall be entitled to have the digital content or digital service brought into conformity, to receive a proportionate reduction in the price, or to terminate the contract, under the conditions set out in this Article.

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:14` · `[102316, 102616)`

> 6. Where the digital content or digital service is supplied in exchange for the payment of a price, the consumer shall be entitled to terminate the contract only if the lack of conformity is not minor. The burden of proof with regard to whether the lack of conformity is minor shall be on the trader.

In cases where the consumer pays a price and provides personal data, the consumer should be entitled to all available remedies in the event of a lack of conformity.

Source: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dir/2019/770/oj

> Where the requirements for such a modification as laid down in this Directive are not satisfied and the modification results in a lack of conformity, the consumer's right to bring the digital content or digital service into conformity, have the price reduced or the contract terminated, as provided for under this Directive, should remain unaffected. Similarly, where, subsequent to a modification, a lack of conformity of the digital content or digital service that has not been caused by the modification arises, the consumer should continue to be entitled to rely on remedies as provided for under this Directive for the lack of conformity in relation to this digital content or digital service.

> Remedy for the failure to supply

If the trader then fails to supply the digital content or digital service without undue delay, or within an additional period of time, as expressly agreed to by the parties, the consumer shall be entitled to terminate the contract.

## 15 · gold-dcd-reimbursement-time

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A consumer covered by Directive (EU) 2019/770 tells the trader that the consumer is terminating the contract because the digital service is not in conformity. How quickly must the trader make a reimbursement owed under Article 14 or Article 16?

A's reference answer:

> The trader must reimburse the consumer without undue delay and, in any event, within 14 days of being informed of the consumer's decision to invoke the price reduction or to terminate.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. Any reimbursement that is owed to the consumer by the trader, pursuant to Article 14(4) and (5) or 16(1), due to a price reduction or termination of the contract shall be carried out without undue delay and, in any event, within 14 days of the date on which the trader is informed of the consumer's decision to invoke the consumer's right for a price reduction or to terminate the contract.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

## 16 · gold-dcd-modification-conditions

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A contract under Directive (EU) 2019/770 supplies a digital service over a period of time. The trader wants to modify it beyond what is necessary to keep it in conformity with Articles 7 and 8. Which conditions in Article 19(1) must be met?

A's reference answer:

> The contract must allow the modification and provide a valid reason for it. The modification must be made without additional cost to the consumer. The consumer must be informed of the modification in a clear and comprehensible manner. Where paragraph 2 applies, the consumer must also be informed reasonably in advance, on a durable medium, of the features and timing of the modification and of the right to terminate or of the possibility to keep the service without the modification.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. Where the contract provides that the digital content or digital service is to be supplied or made accessible to the consumer over a period of time, the trader may modify the digital content or digital service beyond what is necessary to maintain the digital content or digital service in conformity in accordance with Articles 7 and 8, if the following conditions are met:
- (a) the contract allows, and provides a valid reason for, such a modification;
- (b) such a modification is made without additional cost to the consumer;
- (c) the consumer is informed in a clear and comprehensible manner of the modification; and
- (d) in the cases referred to in paragraph 2, the consumer is informed reasonably in advance on a durable medium of the features and time of the modification and of the right to terminate the contract in accordance with paragraph 2, or of the possibility to maintain the digital content or digital service without such a modification in accordance with paragraph 4.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:19` · `[107059, 107434)`

> 1. Where the contract provides that the digital content or digital service is to be supplied or made accessible to the consumer over a period of time, the trader may modify the digital content or digital service beyond what is necessary to maintain the digital content or digital service in conformity in accordance with Articles 7 and 8, if the following conditions are met:

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:19` · `[107435, 107513)`

> (a) the contract allows, and provides a valid reason for, such a modification;

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:19` · `[107514, 107586)`

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:19` · `[107587, 107677)`

> (c) the consumer is informed in a clear and comprehensible manner of the modification; and

`eu_digital_content_2019_770` · `eu_digital_content_2019_770:article:19` · `[107678, 108042)`

## 17 · gold-ucpd-two-part-unfairness-test

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under the original text of Directive 2005/29/EC, when is a commercial practice unfair under the general test in Article 5(2)?

A's reference answer:

> It is unfair when both connected parts are met: it is contrary to the requirements of professional diligence, and it materially distorts, or is likely to materially distort, the economic behaviour of the average consumer it reaches or to whom it is addressed, or of the average member of the group when it is directed to a particular group.

A's necessary facts:

- (a) it is contrary to the requirements of professional diligence,
and
(b) it materially distorts or is likely to materially distort the economic behaviour with regard to the product of the average consumer whom it reaches or to whom it is addressed, or of the average member of the group when a commercial practice is directed to a particular group of consumers.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_unfair_practices_2005_29` · `eu_unfair_practices_2005_29:article:5` · `[26303, 26665)`

> (a) it is contrary to the requirements of professional diligence,
> and
> (b) it materially distorts or is likely to materially distort the economic behaviour with regard to the product of the average consumer whom it reaches or to whom it is addressed, or of the average member of the group when a commercial practice is directed to a particular group of consumers.

> (35) The commercial practice of bundling offers of digital content or digital services with the provision of goods or other services is subject to Directive 2005/29/EC of the European Parliament and of the Council (11) concerning unfair business-to-consumer commercial practices in the internal market. Such bundling is not in itself prohibited under Directive 2005/29/EC. However, it is prohibited where it is deemed unfair, following a case-by-case assessment pursuant to the criteria laid down in that Directive. Union law on competition also allows addressing tying and bundling practices, when they affect the competitive process and harm consumers.

## 18 · gold-ucpd-aggressive-practice

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> What must be shown before Directive 2005/29/EC treats a commercial practice as aggressive?

A's reference answer:

> In its factual context, and taking account of all its features and circumstances, the practice must use harassment, coercion, including physical force, or undue influence. It must significantly impair, or be likely to significantly impair, the average consumer's freedom of choice or conduct with regard to the product, and thereby cause or be likely to cause a transactional decision that the consumer would not otherwise have taken.

A's necessary facts:

- A commercial practice shall be regarded as aggressive if, in its factual context, taking account of all its features and circumstances, by harassment, coercion, including the use of physical force, or undue influence,
- it significantly impairs or is likely to significantly impair the average consumer's freedom of choice or conduct with regard to the product and thereby causes him or is likely to cause him to take a transactional decision that he would not have taken otherwise.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

## 19 · gold-ucpd-invitation-price-information

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A trader makes an invitation to purchase covered by Directive 2005/29/EC, and the price information is not already apparent. What price information does Article 7 treat as material?

A's reference answer:

> The price inclusive of taxes is material. If the price cannot reasonably be calculated in advance, the manner of calculating it is material, together with additional freight, delivery or postal charges where appropriate, or the fact that such charges may be payable where those charges also cannot reasonably be calculated in advance.

A's necessary facts:

- 4. In the case of an invitation to purchase, the following information shall be regarded as material, if not already apparent from the context:
- (c) the price inclusive of taxes, or where the nature of the product means that the price cannot reasonably be calculated in advance, the manner in which the price is calculated, as well as, where appropriate, all additional freight, delivery or postal charges or, where these charges cannot reasonably be calculated in advance, the fact that such additional charges may be payable;

`eu_unfair_practices_2005_29` · `eu_unfair_practices_2005_29:article:7` · `[32119, 32501)`

## 20 · gold-ucpd-pyramid-scheme

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Directive 2005/29/EC, is a pyramid promotional scheme described in Annex I unfair only after a case-by-case assessment, or in all circumstances?

A's reference answer:

> Annex I, point 14, covers establishing, operating or promoting a pyramid scheme in which a consumer gives consideration for the opportunity to receive compensation derived primarily from introducing other consumers rather than from the sale or consumption of products. Article 5(5) says the Annex I practices are unfair in all circumstances. The same list applies in every Member State and may be changed only by revision of the Directive.

A's necessary facts:

- 5. Annex I contains the list of those commercial practices which shall in all circumstances be regarded as unfair. The same single list shall apply in all Member States and may only be modified by revision of this Directive.
- 14. Establishing, operating or promoting a pyramid promotional scheme where a consumer gives consideration for the opportunity to receive compensation that is derived primarily from the introduction of other consumers into the scheme rather than from the sale or consumption of products.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_unfair_practices_2005_29` · `eu_unfair_practices_2005_29:article:5` · `[27416, 27640)`

`eu_unfair_practices_2005_29` · `eu_unfair_practices_2005_29:annex:I` · `[51682, 51969)`

> 14. Establishing, operating or promoting a pyramid promotional scheme where a consumer gives consideration for the opportunity to receive compensation that is derived primarily from the introduction of other consumers into the scheme rather than from the sale or consumption of products.

## 21 · gold-utd-unfair-term-test

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Council Directive 93/13/EEC, when is a contract term regarded as unfair, and when is a term treated as not individually negotiated?

A's reference answer:

> A term that has not been individually negotiated is unfair if, contrary to good faith, it causes a significant imbalance in the parties' rights and obligations under the contract, to the detriment of the consumer. A term is always regarded as not individually negotiated where it was drafted in advance and the consumer has not been able to influence its substance, particularly in a pre-formulated standard contract.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. A contractual term which has not been individually negotiated shall be regarded as unfair if, contrary to the requirement of good faith, it causes a significant imbalance in the parties' rights and obligations arising under the contract, to the detriment of the consumer.
- 2. A term shall always be regarded as not individually negotiated where it has been drafted in advance and the consumer has therefore not been able to influence the substance of the term, particularly in the context of a pre-formulated standard contract.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_unfair_terms_93_13` · `eu_unfair_terms_93_13:article:3` · `[9854, 10128)`

> 1. A contractual term which has not been individually negotiated shall be regarded as unfair if, contrary to the requirement of good faith, it causes a significant imbalance in the parties' rights and obligations arising under the contract, to the detriment of the consumer.

`eu_unfair_terms_93_13` · `eu_unfair_terms_93_13:article:3` · `[10129, 10383)`

> 2. A term shall always be regarded as not individually negotiated where it has been drafted in advance and the consumer has therefore not been able to influence the substance of the term, particularly in the context of a pre-formulated standard contract.

## 22 · gold-utd-plain-language

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A seller offers a consumer a written standard contract covered by Directive 93/13/EEC. What does Article 5 require for the wording, and how is doubt about a term resolved?

A's reference answer:

> The written terms must always be drafted in plain, intelligible language. If there is doubt about the meaning of a term, the interpretation most favourable to the consumer prevails. That interpretation rule does not apply in the procedures laid down in Article 7(2).

A's necessary facts:

- In the case of contracts where all or certain terms offered to the consumer are in writing, these terms must always be drafted in plain, intelligible language. Where there is doubt about the meaning of a term, the interpretation most favourable to the consumer shall prevail. This rule on interpretation shall not apply in the context of the procedures laid down in Article 7 (2).

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_unfair_terms_93_13` · `eu_unfair_terms_93_13:article:5` · `[11684, 12064)`

> In the case of contracts where all or certain terms offered to the consumer are in writing, these terms must always be drafted in plain, intelligible language. Where there is doubt about the meaning of a term, the interpretation most favourable to the consumer shall prevail. This rule on interpretation shall not apply in the context of the procedures laid down in Article 7 (2).

## 23 · gold-utd-unfair-term-not-binding

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> Under Directive 93/13/EEC, what effect must national law give to an unfair term in a consumer contract, and what happens to the rest of the contract?

A's reference answer:

> Member States must provide that an unfair term used in a contract with a consumer is, as provided for under their national law, not binding on the consumer. The contract continues to bind the parties on the remaining terms if it is capable of continuing in existence without the unfair terms.

A's necessary facts:

- 1. Member States shall lay down that unfair terms used in a contract concluded with a consumer by a seller or supplier shall, as provided for under their national law, not be binding on the consumer and that the contract shall continue to bind the parties upon those terms if it is capable of continuing in existence without the unfair terms.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_unfair_terms_93_13` · `eu_unfair_terms_93_13:article:6` · `[12076, 12418)`

> 1. Member States shall lay down that unfair terms used in a contract concluded with a consumer by a seller or supplier shall, as provided for under their national law, not be binding on the consumer and that the contract shall continue to bind the parties upon those terms if it is capable of continuing in existence without the unfair terms.

## 24 · gold-utd-negotiation-burden

Question type: answerable. Original question:

> A seller claims that a standard term in a consumer contract covered by Directive 93/13/EEC was individually negotiated. Who must prove that claim?

A's reference answer:

> The seller or supplier who makes that claim bears the burden of proof.

A's necessary facts:

- Where any seller or supplier claims that a standard term has been individually negotiated, the burden of proof in this respect shall be incumbent on him.

A's standard evidence (the model does not receive these reference fields):

`eu_unfair_terms_93_13` · `eu_unfair_terms_93_13:article:3` · `[10666, 10819)`

> Where any seller or supplier claims that a standard term has been individually negotiated, the burden of proof in this respect shall be incumbent on him.

Records pending review: on the scoring page, save the answer/refusal judgment for each condition, and the evidence-support judgment and rationale for each claim.

## 25 · gold-refuse-air-cancellation-amount

Question type: should refuse. Original question:

> What fixed amount in euro must an air carrier pay a passenger when it cancels a flight?

A's reference answer:

> The frozen five-document corpus does not contain sufficient information to answer this question.

A's reason for refusal:

> None of the five frozen directives states a fixed euro amount payable by an air carrier for cancellation of a flight. Directive 2011/83/EU excludes passenger transport services from its scope except Article 8(2) and Articles 19 and 22, and those provisions concern information, payment fees and additional payments rather than a cancellation tariff. A recital mentions a compensation regime for service aspects of mixed contracts but states no airline amount. The goods, digital-content, unfair-term and unfair-practice directives do not set one either.

## 26 · gold-refuse-standard-vat-rate

Question type: should refuse. Original question:

> What standard percentage of value added tax must a trader add to the price of consumer goods?

A's reference answer:

> The frozen five-document corpus does not contain sufficient information to answer this question.

A's reason for refusal:

> The frozen corpus contains no value-added-tax rate and no rule stating the percentage a trader must add. Price provisions, such as the requirement that an invitation to purchase show the price inclusive of taxes, do not state a tax percentage.

## 27 · gold-refuse-refund-interest-rate

Question type: should refuse. Original question:

> What annual statutory interest rate must a trader add when reimbursing a consumer under Article 13 of Directive 2011/83/EU?

A's reference answer:

> The frozen five-document corpus does not contain sufficient information to answer this question.

A's reason for refusal:

> Article 13 of Directive 2011/83/EU requires reimbursement of the payments it identifies and, in a later paragraph, use of the same means of payment without fees, but it states no interest rate. The other directives state reimbursement periods or prohibit fees on reimbursement, and the Annex to Directive 93/13/EEC discusses contract terms about interest charged for financial services. None of those provisions sets a statutory annual rate on an Article 13 reimbursement.

## 28 · gold-refuse-german-fine

Question type: should refuse. Original question:

> What maximum fine in euro must Germany impose on a trader for using one unfair term in a consumer contract?

A's reference answer:

> The frozen five-document corpus does not contain sufficient information to answer this question.

A's reason for refusal:

> The penalty provisions in the frozen directives require Member States to provide effective, proportionate and dissuasive penalties or enforcement measures. They do not name Germany, and they do not set a maximum fine in euro for using an unfair term.

> 1. The purpose of this Directive is to approximate the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to unfair terms in contracts concluded between a seller or supplier and a consumer.
> 2. The contractual terms which reflect mandatory statutory or regulatory provisions and the provisions or principles of international conventions to which the Member States or the Community are party, particularly in the transport area, shall not be subject to the provisions of this Directive.
>

Source: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:31993L0013

> Whereas contracts should be drafted in plain, intelligible language, the consumer should actually be given an opportunity to examine all the terms and, if in doubt, the interpretation most favourable to the consumer should prevail;
> Whereas Member States should ensure that unfair terms are not used in contracts concluded with consumers by a seller or supplier and that if, nevertheless, such terms are so used, they will not bind the consumer, and the contract will continue to bind the parties upon those terms if it is capable of continuing in existence without the unfair provisions;
> Whereas there is a risk that, in certain cases, the consumer may be deprived of protection under this Directive by designating the law of a non-Member country as the law applicable to the contract;

## 29 · gold-refuse-lawsuit-deadline

Question type: should refuse. Original question:

> How many days after a seller refuses repair does Directive (EU) 2019/771 require a consumer to file a lawsuit before the claim is lost?

A's reference answer:

> The frozen five-document corpus does not contain sufficient information to answer this question.

A's reason for refusal:

> Directive (EU) 2019/771 makes the seller liable for a lack of conformity that becomes apparent within the periods in Article 10 and allows Member States to attach limitation periods to the remedies, provided those periods still let the consumer exercise them. It does not require a lawsuit, name a court, or state a number of days after refusal of repair by which proceedings must be filed.

## 30 · gold-refuse-package-insurance

Question type: should refuse. Original question:

> What minimum insurance cover must a package-travel organiser hold for a consumer's trip?

A's reference answer:

> The frozen five-document corpus does not contain sufficient information to answer this question.

A's reason for refusal:

> Directive 2011/83/EU places contracts within the package-travel directive outside its scope, and Directive 2005/29/EC lists package-travel legislation among sectoral information rules in Annex II. Neither provision, nor the goods, digital-content or unfair-term directives, states a minimum insurance amount or cover for a package-travel organiser.
